Well, I suppose... it's just that I can't seem to turn off the worrying once it starts. Even when I tell myself, you know, I've made the list, I've checked all the dates, I've done everything I reasonably can. I've always been a bit of a planner, you see. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, constantly. It's never just one thing, you see. My mind just keeps roaming all over the place. Like yesterday, I started out fretting over the exhibition schedule at the historical society. I volunteer there on Tuesdays, you know, and we're putting together a lovely display of vintage textiles. But the curator, David, he's terribly slow at finalizing the display cards. And while I was thinking about that, suddenly I'm worrying about my daughter's travel plans. She's flying out next month, and you hear such dreadful things about lost luggage these days. My neighbor lost her suitcase in Rome and had to wear the same blouse for a week. A yellow blouse, too, which really washed her out, bless her heart. 

But anyway, then I'm moving right along to thinking about whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains, because the oak tree in the front yard drops so many leaves. It's just... small things, you know? None of it is a real emergency. But my brain just grabs them, and they start to feel as if they could unravel into something much, much bigger. I don't even know why I get so worked up half the time.

Well, I suppose I do... yes, the restlessness is quite noticeable. I'll be sitting right in my own living room, and I just feel so wound up. Like when I'm on the phone with my sister, Eleanor—I can't just sit still. I'm always tapping my fingers on the armrest, or I have to get up and straighten a picture frame or adjust the curtains. Sitting quietly just makes me feel terribly jumpy. 

Eleanor is the same way, actually, though she’d never admit it. She’s always fussing over her hydrangeas. Oh, you should see her garden in the summer, it's magnificent. The blue ones, especially. She uses those coffee grounds to change the soil acidity, you know. I tried it once with my rhododendrons, but I don't think I drink enough coffee to make a difference. I mostly drink tea now. Earl Grey, usually. Though the last box I bought tasted a bit dusty. I wonder if they changed the bergamot supplier... but yes, what were we saying? Oh, the jumpiness. Yes, I just can't seem to settle down in my own house.

I wonder if they changed the bergamot supplier... but yes, what were we saying? Oh, the jumpiness. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I wouldn't say I have... rituals, exactly. Or those intrusive thoughts you mentioned. It's really just the worrying, like I said. The fretting over things that might go wrong. I don't feel like I *have* to do things a certain way to stop the thoughts, like... like turning a doorknob three times or anything like that. 

My cousin Arthur used to do things like that, you know. He had to check the stove was off exactly five times before he could leave the house. Every single time. It made him terribly late for my wedding. He missed the entire ceremony and only showed up for the reception, and even then, he was sweating profusely. Of course, it was July, and the air conditioning at the country club was on the fritz. They told us it would be fixed by the weekend, but you know how contractors are. Always promising the moon and delivering a puddle. We had to open all the windows, and then the mosquitoes got in. My poor mother had a bite right on her nose for all the photographs. She tried to cover it with powder, but it just made it look like a little pink mountain... but no, I don't have anything like Arthur had. Just the regular worrying, I suppose.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's never just one thing, you see. My mind just keeps roaming all over the place. Like yesterday, I started out fretting over the exhibition schedule at the historical society. I volunteer there on Tuesdays, you know, and we're putting together a lovely display of vintage textiles. But the curator, David, he's terribly slow at finalizing the display cards. And while I was thinking about that, suddenly I'm worrying about my daughter's travel plans. She's flying out next month, and you hear such dreadful things about lost luggage these days. My neighbor lost her suitcase in Rome and had to wear the same blouse for a week. A yellow blouse, too, which really washed her out, bless her heart. \n\nBut anyway, then I'm moving right along to thinking about whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains, because the oak tree in the front yard drops so many leaves. It's just... small things, you know? None of it is a real emergency. But my brain just grabs them, and they start to feel as if they could unravel into something much, much bigger. I don't even know why I get so worked up half the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, or trouble sleeping because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do... yes, the restlessness is quite noticeable. I'll be sitting right in my own living room, and I just feel so wound up. Like when I'm on the phone with my sister, Eleanor—I can't just sit still. I'm always tapping my fingers on the armrest, or I have to get up and straighten a picture frame or adjust the curtains. Sitting quietly just makes me feel terribly jumpy. \n\nEleanor is the same way, actually, though she’d never admit it. She’s always fussing over her hydrangeas. Oh, you should see her garden in the summer, it's magnificent. The blue ones, especially. She uses those coffee grounds to change the soil acidity, you know. I tried it once with my rhododendrons, but I don't think I drink enough coffee to make a difference. I mostly drink tea now. Earl Grey, usually. Though the last box I bought tasted a bit dusty. I wonder if they changed the bergamot supplier... but yes, what were we saying? Oh, the jumpiness. Yes, I just can't seem to settle down in my own house.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that cause you significant distress, or do you feel driven to perform certain behaviors or mental rituals to reduce the anxiety caused by these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I have... rituals, exactly. Or those intrusive thoughts you mentioned. It's really just the worrying, like I said. The fretting over things that might go wrong. I don't feel like I *have* to do things a certain way to stop the thoughts, like... like turning a doorknob three times or anything like that. \n\nMy cousin Arthur used to do things like that, you know. He had to check the stove was off exactly five times before he could leave the house. Every single time. It made him terribly late for my wedding. He missed the entire ceremony and only showed up for the reception, and even then, he was sweating profusely. Of course, it was July, and the air conditioning at the country club was on the fritz. They told us it would be fixed by the weekend, but you know how contractors are. Always promising the moon and delivering a puddle. We had to open all the windows, and then the mosquitoes got in. My poor mother had a bite right on her nose for all the photographs. She tried to cover it with powder, but it just made it look like a little pink mountain... but no, I don't have anything like Arthur had. Just the regular worrying, I suppose.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your worrying and feeling anxious happens more often than not, and has been going on like this for at least six months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about seven months, since shortly after my friend died and I tried to keep our projects moving.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, oh yes, definitely more often than not. I'd say it's been about seven months now. It all really started after my dear friend Margaret passed away. We were co-chairs of the historical society's annual gala, you see, and I just tried to keep all our little projects moving forward after she was gone. Margaret was always the organized one. She had this wonderful system of color-coded binders. Red for catering, blue for decorations, green for the silent auction... 

Oh, the silent auction! Last year, someone donated a week at their timeshare in Boca Raton, and you wouldn't believe the bidding war between Mrs. Gable and the new dentist in town. Dr. Peterson, I think his name is. Very nice young man, though he did suggest I might need a night guard for my teeth. I told him I don't grind my teeth, but he said the enamel tells a different story. I suppose he'd know, being a dentist and all. He has such a lovely office, too—they play that very soothing classical music, cellos mostly. I've always loved the cello. My nephew tried to learn the cello in middle school, but the instrument was bigger than he was! He looked like a little turtle carrying its shell around. He gave it up for the clarinet eventually. Much easier to carry on the school bus. But yes, seven months of this constant worrying, ever since Margaret... I just haven't been able to turn my brain off since then.

I wonder if they changed the bergamot supplier... but yes, what were we saying? Oh, the jumpiness. Yes, I just can't seem to settle down in my own house.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that cause you significant distress, or do you feel driven to perform certain behaviors or mental rituals to reduce the anxiety caused by these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I have... rituals, exactly. Or those intrusive thoughts you mentioned. It's really just the worrying, like I said. The fretting over things that might go wrong. I don't feel like I *have* to do things a certain way to stop the thoughts, like... like turning a doorknob three times or anything like that. \n\nMy cousin Arthur used to do things like that, you know. He had to check the stove was off exactly five times before he could leave the house. Every single time. It made him terribly late for my wedding. He missed the entire ceremony and only showed up for the reception, and even then, he was sweating profusely. Of course, it was July, and the air conditioning at the country club was on the fritz. They told us it would be fixed by the weekend, but you know how contractors are. Always promising the moon and delivering a puddle. We had to open all the windows, and then the mosquitoes got in. My poor mother had a bite right on her nose for all the photographs. She tried to cover it with powder, but it just made it look like a little pink mountain... but no, I don't have anything like Arthur had. Just the regular worrying, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying and feeling anxious happens more often than not, and has been going on like this for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, oh yes, definitely more often than not. I'd say it's been about seven months now. It all really started after my dear friend Margaret passed away. We were co-chairs of the historical society's annual gala, you see, and I just tried to keep all our little projects moving forward after she was gone. Margaret was always the organized one. She had this wonderful system of color-coded binders. Red for catering, blue for decorations, green for the silent auction... \n\nOh, the silent auction! Last year, someone donated a week at their timeshare in Boca Raton, and you wouldn't believe the bidding war between Mrs. Gable and the new dentist in town. Dr. Peterson, I think his name is. Very nice young man, though he did suggest I might need a night guard for my teeth. I told him I don't grind my teeth, but he said the enamel tells a different story. I suppose he'd know, being a dentist and all. He has such a lovely office, too—they play that very soothing classical music, cellos mostly. I've always loved the cello. My nephew tried to learn the cello in middle school, but the instrument was bigger than he was! He looked like a little turtle carrying its shell around. He gave it up for the clarinet eventually. Much easier to carry on the school bus. But yes, seven months of this constant worrying, ever since Margaret... I just haven't been able to turn my brain off since then.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that your anxiety and worry are persistent and occur more days than not, even when there isn't an immediate reason for concern?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My mind keeps roaming from the exhibition schedule to my daughter's travel plans to whether the house gutters need fixing, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose it is like that. Even when things are perfectly fine, my mind just... keeps roaming. One minute I'm thinking about the historical society's exhibition schedule, and the next I'm fretting over my daughter's travel plans, or whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains. And it's like... these small things just start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger, you know? 

Speaking of gutters, the man who came to clean them last year, Mr. Henderson—oh, he made such a mess of the flower beds right below the porch. Stepped right on my prize-winning begonias! I was so upset, I almost called his supervisor, but he was just a young boy, really. Looked like he couldn't have been more than nineteen. He told me he was saving up for community college. Wants to study marine biology, of all things. In Ohio! I asked him how he planned to study the ocean in the middle of the Midwest, and he said something about freshwater ecosystems. Which I suppose makes sense, with the lake right there. We used to go up to the lake every summer when the children were small. My husband would rent this little pontoon boat... oh, it was so peaceful. But now, yes, even when I'm just sitting quietly, the worry is just... always there, most days, even without a good reason.

She had this wonderful system of color-coded binders. Red for catering, blue for decorations, green for the silent auction... \n\nOh, the silent auction! Last year, someone donated a week at their timeshare in Boca Raton, and you wouldn't believe the bidding war between Mrs. Gable and the new dentist in town. Dr. Peterson, I think his name is. Very nice young man, though he did suggest I might need a night guard for my teeth. I told him I don't grind my teeth, but he said the enamel tells a different story. I suppose he'd know, being a dentist and all. He has such a lovely office, too—they play that very soothing classical music, cellos mostly. I've always loved the cello. My nephew tried to learn the cello in middle school, but the instrument was bigger than he was! He looked like a little turtle carrying its shell around. He gave it up for the clarinet eventually. Much easier to carry on the school bus. But yes, seven months of this constant worrying, ever since Margaret... I just haven't been able to turn my brain off since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety and worry are persistent and occur more days than not, even when there isn't an immediate reason for concern?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is like that. Even when things are perfectly fine, my mind just... keeps roaming. One minute I'm thinking about the historical society's exhibition schedule, and the next I'm fretting over my daughter's travel plans, or whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains. And it's like... these small things just start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger, you know? \n\nSpeaking of gutters, the man who came to clean them last year, Mr. Henderson—oh, he made such a mess of the flower beds right below the porch. Stepped right on my prize-winning begonias! I was so upset, I almost called his supervisor, but he was just a young boy, really. Looked like he couldn't have been more than nineteen. He told me he was saving up for community college. Wants to study marine biology, of all things. In Ohio! I asked him how he planned to study the ocean in the middle of the Midwest, and he said something about freshwater ecosystems. Which I suppose makes sense, with the lake right there. We used to go up to the lake every summer when the children were small. My husband would rent this little pontoon boat... oh, it was so peaceful. But now, yes, even when I'm just sitting quietly, the worry is just... always there, most days, even without a good reason.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas this constant worrying started to affect your daily life in significant ways, like making it hard to focus on tasks, causing problems at work or home, or affecting your relationships?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it hasn't completely stopped me from doing things, but... it's definitely making things harder. I still keep up with my garden, of course, and the weekly book club. But I've noticed I'm just arriving less prepared. Last week we were reading that new historical fiction novel about the Tudor court, and I barely made it through the first three chapters. Usually, I have notes! 

And with the historical society... I've actually started turning down parts of the exhibition planning. It just feels so much harder to manage than it used to. I told them I couldn't handle the catering coordination this time around. Which is a shame, because I always use that lovely bakery on Elm Street. The one run by the two sisters? Oh, they make the most divine miniature fruit tarts. The pastry is so flaky, it just melts in your mouth. They use a special French butter, I believe. One of the sisters, Eleanor, went to culinary school in Paris. She met her husband there, a very handsome man who imports wine. They had a beautiful wedding in a chateau, or so I heard from Mrs. Gable. Mrs. Gable's daughter went to school with Eleanor, you see. Small world, isn't it? But yes, the planning just feels overwhelming now, so I've had to step back from some of my usual responsibilities.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I'm not entirely sure what you mean by that. I mean, my worries are just... my worries, you know? They're all sort of jumbled up together in my head. I suppose they might seem disconnected to someone else, but to me, they're all just things that need my attention. 

Like yesterday, I was standing in line at the post office. I had to mail a package to my sister in Seattle—she's just had her kitchen remodeled, and I found the most darling set of vintage tea towels that match her new backsplash perfectly. Seafoam green, she went with. Very daring, I thought, but it looks lovely with the white cabinets. Anyway, I was standing there, and suddenly I started worrying about whether I'd left the iron on. I hadn't even been ironing! But then that made me think about my husband's dress shirts, and how the dry cleaner on Main Street always uses too much starch. The owner, Mr. Kim, is a sweetheart, though. He always gives my grandson a lollipop when we go in. Cherry, usually. Though last time it was grape, and little Tommy ended up with a purple tongue for hours! My daughter was not pleased, let me tell you. She's very strict about artificial dyes. Only natural fruit juices for her kids. Which I understand, I really do, but sometimes a little treat... 

Oh, but you were asking about my worries shifting. I just... I don't know if they shift easily, or if they're just always there, all at once. I just haven't really noticed them jumping from one thing to another like that. It's more of a constant hum, really.

Sudden episodes? Like out of nowhere? No, I don't think so. I mean, my heart doesn't just start racing from fear or anything like that. The only time I really get sweaty or short of breath is if I'm out in the garden and it's terribly humid. 

You know, like last July? Oh, it was just dreadful. We had that heatwave that lasted nearly three weeks. My poor hydrangeas were practically crisp by the end of it. I tried watering them twice a day, early morning and dusk, just like the Farmer's Almanac says to do. My grandfather swore by the Almanac. He used to farm corn down in southern Illinois. Hundreds of acres. He had this old green tractor that he'd let us ride on when we visited as children. It was so loud, it would make your teeth rattle, and the smell of the exhaust... well, it's funny the things you remember, isn't it? My brother actually tried to drive it once when he was twelve and nearly took out a fence post. My grandfather was furious, but he calmed down after a slice of my grandmother's rhubarb pie. 

But no, to answer your question... I haven't had any sudden spells of terror or anything physical like that. Just that constant, quiet worry I mentioned before.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... it's just that I can't seem to turn off the worrying once it starts. Even when I tell myself, you know, I've made the list, I've checked all the dates, I've done everything I reasonably can. I've always been a bit of a planner, you see. Even back when I was working at the university—oh, the filing system I had there, it was a marvel. Everyone said so. My supervisor, Brenda, she used to say, \"Martha, if the building burns down, your desk will be the only thing organized enough to survive.\" She was a lovely woman, Brenda. Moved to Florida a few years ago. Or was it Arizona? Somewhere warm. I meant to send her a card for Christmas, but then the postage rates went up again, and I got so distracted trying to find my book of stamps... but yes, anyway, the worrying. It just... it doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's never just one thing, you see. My mind just keeps roaming all over the place. Like yesterday, I started out fretting over the exhibition schedule at the historical society. I volunteer there on Tuesdays, you know, and we're putting together a lovely display of vintage textiles. But the curator, David, he's terribly slow at finalizing the display cards. And while I was thinking about that, suddenly I'm worrying about my daughter's travel plans. She's flying out next month, and you hear such dreadful things about lost luggage these days. My neighbor lost her suitcase in Rome and had to wear the same blouse for a week. A yellow blouse, too, which really washed her out, bless her heart. \n\nBut anyway, then I'm moving right along to thinking about whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains, because the oak tree in the front yard drops so many leaves. It's just... small things, you know? None of it is a real emergency. But my brain just grabs them, and they start to feel as if they could unravel into something much, much bigger. I don't even know why I get so worked up half the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, or trouble sleeping because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do... yes, the restlessness is quite noticeable. I'll be sitting right in my own living room, and I just feel so wound up. Like when I'm on the phone with my sister, Eleanor—I can't just sit still. I'm always tapping my fingers on the armrest, or I have to get up and straighten a picture frame or adjust the curtains. Sitting quietly just makes me feel terribly jumpy. \n\nEleanor is the same way, actually, though she’d never admit it. She’s always fussing over her hydrangeas. Oh, you should see her garden in the summer, it's magnificent. The blue ones, especially. She uses those coffee grounds to change the soil acidity, you know. I tried it once with my rhododendrons, but I don't think I drink enough coffee to make a difference. I mostly drink tea now. Earl Grey, usually. Though the last box I bought tasted a bit dusty. I wonder if they changed the bergamot supplier... but yes, what were we saying? Oh, the jumpiness. Yes, I just can't seem to settle down in my own house.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that cause you significant distress, or do you feel driven to perform certain behaviors or mental rituals to reduce the anxiety caused by these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I have... rituals, exactly. Or those intrusive thoughts you mentioned. It's really just the worrying, like I said. The fretting over things that might go wrong. I don't feel like I *have* to do things a certain way to stop the thoughts, like... like turning a doorknob three times or anything like that. \n\nMy cousin Arthur used to do things like that, you know. He had to check the stove was off exactly five times before he could leave the house. Every single time. It made him terribly late for my wedding. He missed the entire ceremony and only showed up for the reception, and even then, he was sweating profusely. Of course, it was July, and the air conditioning at the country club was on the fritz. They told us it would be fixed by the weekend, but you know how contractors are. Always promising the moon and delivering a puddle. We had to open all the windows, and then the mosquitoes got in. My poor mother had a bite right on her nose for all the photographs. She tried to cover it with powder, but it just made it look like a little pink mountain... but no, I don't have anything like Arthur had. Just the regular worrying, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying and feeling anxious happens more often than not, and has been going on like this for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, oh yes, definitely more often than not. I'd say it's been about seven months now. It all really started after my dear friend Margaret passed away. We were co-chairs of the historical society's annual gala, you see, and I just tried to keep all our little projects moving forward after she was gone. Margaret was always the organized one. She had this wonderful system of color-coded binders. Red for catering, blue for decorations, green for the silent auction... \n\nOh, the silent auction! Last year, someone donated a week at their timeshare in Boca Raton, and you wouldn't believe the bidding war between Mrs. Gable and the new dentist in town. Dr. Peterson, I think his name is. Very nice young man, though he did suggest I might need a night guard for my teeth. I told him I don't grind my teeth, but he said the enamel tells a different story. I suppose he'd know, being a dentist and all. He has such a lovely office, too—they play that very soothing classical music, cellos mostly. I've always loved the cello. My nephew tried to learn the cello in middle school, but the instrument was bigger than he was! He looked like a little turtle carrying its shell around. He gave it up for the clarinet eventually. Much easier to carry on the school bus. But yes, seven months of this constant worrying, ever since Margaret... I just haven't been able to turn my brain off since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety and worry are persistent and occur more days than not, even when there isn't an immediate reason for concern?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is like that. Even when things are perfectly fine, my mind just... keeps roaming. One minute I'm thinking about the historical society's exhibition schedule, and the next I'm fretting over my daughter's travel plans, or whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains. And it's like... these small things just start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger, you know? \n\nSpeaking of gutters, the man who came to clean them last year, Mr. Henderson—oh, he made such a mess of the flower beds right below the porch. Stepped right on my prize-winning begonias! I was so upset, I almost called his supervisor, but he was just a young boy, really. Looked like he couldn't have been more than nineteen. He told me he was saving up for community college. Wants to study marine biology, of all things. In Ohio! I asked him how he planned to study the ocean in the middle of the Midwest, and he said something about freshwater ecosystems. Which I suppose makes sense, with the lake right there. We used to go up to the lake every summer when the children were small. My husband would rent this little pontoon boat... oh, it was so peaceful. But now, yes, even when I'm just sitting quietly, the worry is just... always there, most days, even without a good reason.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worrying started to affect your daily life in significant ways, like making it hard to focus on tasks, causing problems at work or home, or affecting your relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't completely stopped me from doing things, but... it's definitely making things harder. I still keep up with my garden, of course, and the weekly book club. But I've noticed I'm just arriving less prepared. Last week we were reading that new historical fiction novel about the Tudor court, and I barely made it through the first three chapters. Usually, I have notes! \n\nAnd with the historical society... I've actually started turning down parts of the exhibition planning. It just feels so much harder to manage than it used to. I told them I couldn't handle the catering coordination this time around. Which is a shame, because I always use that lovely bakery on Elm Street. The one run by the two sisters? Oh, they make the most divine miniature fruit tarts. The pastry is so flaky, it just melts in your mouth. They use a special French butter, I believe. One of the sisters, Eleanor, went to culinary school in Paris. She met her husband there, a very handsome man who imports wine. They had a beautiful wedding in a chateau, or so I heard from Mrs. Gable. Mrs. Gable's daughter went to school with Eleanor, you see. Small world, isn't it? But yes, the planning just feels overwhelming now, so I've had to step back from some of my usual responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying tends to shift from one concern to another easily, even when those things aren't necessarily connected?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure what you mean by that. I mean, my worries are just... my worries, you know? They're all sort of jumbled up together in my head. I suppose they might seem disconnected to someone else, but to me, they're all just things that need my attention. \n\nLike yesterday, I was standing in line at the post office. I had to mail a package to my sister in Seattle—she's just had her kitchen remodeled, and I found the most darling set of vintage tea towels that match her new backsplash perfectly. Seafoam green, she went with. Very daring, I thought, but it looks lovely with the white cabinets. Anyway, I was standing there, and suddenly I started worrying about whether I'd left the iron on. I hadn't even been ironing! But then that made me think about my husband's dress shirts, and how the dry cleaner on Main Street always uses too much starch. The owner, Mr. Kim, is a sweetheart, though. He always gives my grandson a lollipop when we go in. Cherry, usually. Though last time it was grape, and little Tommy ended up with a purple tongue for hours! My daughter was not pleased, let me tell you. She's very strict about artificial dyes. Only natural fruit juices for her kids. Which I understand, I really do, but sometimes a little treat... \n\nOh, but you were asking about my worries shifting. I just... I don't know if they shift easily, or if they're just always there, all at once. I just haven't really noticed them jumping from one thing to another like that. It's more of a constant hum, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort that came on very quickly and included physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling short of breath?\n\nPatient: Sudden episodes? Like out of nowhere? No, I don't think so. I mean, my heart doesn't just start racing from fear or anything like that. The only time I really get sweaty or short of breath is if I'm out in the garden and it's terribly humid. \n\nYou know, like last July? Oh, it was just dreadful. We had that heatwave that lasted nearly three weeks. My poor hydrangeas were practically crisp by the end of it. I tried watering them twice a day, early morning and dusk, just like the Farmer's Almanac says to do. My grandfather swore by the Almanac. He used to farm corn down in southern Illinois. Hundreds of acres. He had this old green tractor that he'd let us ride on when we visited as children. It was so loud, it would make your teeth rattle, and the smell of the exhaust... well, it's funny the things you remember, isn't it? My brother actually tried to drive it once when he was twelve and nearly took out a fence post. My grandfather was furious, but he calmed down after a slice of my grandmother's rhubarb pie. \n\nBut no, to answer your question... I haven't had any sudden spells of terror or anything physical like that. Just that constant, quiet worry I mentioned before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F41.1", "F42.0"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

Only natural fruit juices for her kids. Which I understand, I really do, but sometimes a little treat... \n\nOh, but you were asking about my worries shifting. I just... I don't know if they shift easily, or if they're just always there, all at once. I just haven't really noticed them jumping from one thing to another like that. It's more of a constant hum, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort that came on very quickly and included physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling short of breath?\n\nPatient: Sudden episodes? Like out of nowhere? No, I don't think so. I mean, my heart doesn't just start racing from fear or anything like that. The only time I really get sweaty or short of breath is if I'm out in the garden and it's terribly humid. \n\nYou know, like last July? Oh, it was just dreadful. We had that heatwave that lasted nearly three weeks. My poor hydrangeas were practically crisp by the end of it. I tried watering them twice a day, early morning and dusk, just like the Farmer's Almanac says to do. My grandfather swore by the Almanac. He used to farm corn down in southern Illinois. Hundreds of acres. He had this old green tractor that he'd let us ride on when we visited as children. It was so loud, it would make your teeth rattle, and the smell of the exhaust... well, it's funny the things you remember, isn't it? My brother actually tried to drive it once when he was twelve and nearly took out a fence post. My grandfather was furious, but he calmed down after a slice of my grandmother's rhubarb pie. \n\nBut no, to answer your question... I haven't had any sudden spells of terror or anything physical like that. Just that constant, quiet worry I mentioned before.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel tense in your muscles, like your body is stiff or tight, especially when you're not doing anything physically strenuous?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, actually. I do get terribly tight. By the time evening rolls around, my shoulders and my neck are just... they're so stiff. It's like I've been holding myself braced all day long without even realizing it. I'll be sitting there in my armchair—it's a lovely wingback, reupholstered just last year in this beautiful floral chintz. I found the fabric at that little shop downtown, the one next to the antique store. The owner, Margaret, has the most exquisite taste. She travels to England every spring to source her textiles. She brought back this gorgeous William Morris print once, but it was far too expensive for my budget. My daughter said I should have splurged, but you know how it is. You have to be sensible. 

Anyway, I'll be sitting in that chair, trying to read or, you know, plan out the activities for the garden center—we're doing a whole workshop on drought-resistant perennials next month, which I think is so important with the summers getting hotter—and I'll suddenly realize I'm clenching my jaw. Just biting down so hard. It gives me a bit of a headache sometimes, right at the temples. But yes, my muscles do get very tense, even when I'm just sitting quietly.

They told us it would be fixed by the weekend, but you know how contractors are. Always promising the moon and delivering a puddle. We had to open all the windows, and then the mosquitoes got in. My poor mother had a bite right on her nose for all the photographs. She tried to cover it with powder, but it just made it look like a little pink mountain... but no, I don't have anything like Arthur had. Just the regular worrying, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying and feeling anxious happens more often than not, and has been going on like this for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, oh yes, definitely more often than not. I'd say it's been about seven months now. It all really started after my dear friend Margaret passed away. We were co-chairs of the historical society's annual gala, you see, and I just tried to keep all our little projects moving forward after she was gone. Margaret was always the organized one. She had this wonderful system of color-coded binders. Red for catering, blue for decorations, green for the silent auction... \n\nOh, the silent auction! Last year, someone donated a week at their timeshare in Boca Raton, and you wouldn't believe the bidding war between Mrs. Gable and the new dentist in town. Dr. Peterson, I think his name is. Very nice young man, though he did suggest I might need a night guard for my teeth. I told him I don't grind my teeth, but he said the enamel tells a different story. I suppose he'd know, being a dentist and all. He has such a lovely office, too—they play that very soothing classical music, cellos mostly. I've always loved the cello. My nephew tried to learn the cello in middle school, but the instrument was bigger than he was! He looked like a little turtle carrying its shell around. He gave it up for the clarinet eventually. Much easier to carry on the school bus. But yes, seven months of this constant worrying, ever since Margaret... I just haven't been able to turn my brain off since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety and worry are persistent and occur more days than not, even when there isn't an immediate reason for concern?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is like that. Even when things are perfectly fine, my mind just... keeps roaming. One minute I'm thinking about the historical society's exhibition schedule, and the next I'm fretting over my daughter's travel plans, or whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains. And it's like... these small things just start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger, you know? \n\nSpeaking of gutters, the man who came to clean them last year, Mr. Henderson—oh, he made such a mess of the flower beds right below the porch. Stepped right on my prize-winning begonias! I was so upset, I almost called his supervisor, but he was just a young boy, really. Looked like he couldn't have been more than nineteen. He told me he was saving up for community college. Wants to study marine biology, of all things. In Ohio! I asked him how he planned to study the ocean in the middle of the Midwest, and he said something about freshwater ecosystems. Which I suppose makes sense, with the lake right there. We used to go up to the lake every summer when the children were small. My husband would rent this little pontoon boat... oh, it was so peaceful. But now, yes, even when I'm just sitting quietly, the worry is just... always there, most days, even without a good reason.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worrying started to affect your daily life in significant ways, like making it hard to focus on tasks, causing problems at work or home, or affecting your relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't completely stopped me from doing things, but... it's definitely making things harder. I still keep up with my garden, of course, and the weekly book club. But I've noticed I'm just arriving less prepared. Last week we were reading that new historical fiction novel about the Tudor court, and I barely made it through the first three chapters. Usually, I have notes! \n\nAnd with the historical society... I've actually started turning down parts of the exhibition planning. It just feels so much harder to manage than it used to. I told them I couldn't handle the catering coordination this time around. Which is a shame, because I always use that lovely bakery on Elm Street. The one run by the two sisters? Oh, they make the most divine miniature fruit tarts. The pastry is so flaky, it just melts in your mouth. They use a special French butter, I believe. One of the sisters, Eleanor, went to culinary school in Paris. She met her husband there, a very handsome man who imports wine. They had a beautiful wedding in a chateau, or so I heard from Mrs. Gable. Mrs. Gable's daughter went to school with Eleanor, you see. Small world, isn't it? But yes, the planning just feels overwhelming now, so I've had to step back from some of my usual responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying tends to shift from one concern to another easily, even when those things aren't necessarily connected?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure what you mean by that. I mean, my worries are just... my worries, you know? They're all sort of jumbled up together in my head. I suppose they might seem disconnected to someone else, but to me, they're all just things that need my attention. \n\nLike yesterday, I was standing in line at the post office. I had to mail a package to my sister in Seattle—she's just had her kitchen remodeled, and I found the most darling set of vintage tea towels that match her new backsplash perfectly. Seafoam green, she went with. Very daring, I thought, but it looks lovely with the white cabinets. Anyway, I was standing there, and suddenly I started worrying about whether I'd left the iron on. I hadn't even been ironing! But then that made me think about my husband's dress shirts, and how the dry cleaner on Main Street always uses too much starch. The owner, Mr. Kim, is a sweetheart, though. He always gives my grandson a lollipop when we go in. Cherry, usually. Though last time it was grape, and little Tommy ended up with a purple tongue for hours! My daughter was not pleased, let me tell you. She's very strict about artificial dyes. Only natural fruit juices for her kids. Which I understand, I really do, but sometimes a little treat... \n\nOh, but you were asking about my worries shifting. I just... I don't know if they shift easily, or if they're just always there, all at once. I just haven't really noticed them jumping from one thing to another like that. It's more of a constant hum, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort that came on very quickly and included physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling short of breath?\n\nPatient: Sudden episodes? Like out of nowhere? No, I don't think so. I mean, my heart doesn't just start racing from fear or anything like that. The only time I really get sweaty or short of breath is if I'm out in the garden and it's terribly humid. \n\nYou know, like last July? Oh, it was just dreadful. We had that heatwave that lasted nearly three weeks. My poor hydrangeas were practically crisp by the end of it. I tried watering them twice a day, early morning and dusk, just like the Farmer's Almanac says to do. My grandfather swore by the Almanac. He used to farm corn down in southern Illinois. Hundreds of acres. He had this old green tractor that he'd let us ride on when we visited as children. It was so loud, it would make your teeth rattle, and the smell of the exhaust... well, it's funny the things you remember, isn't it? My brother actually tried to drive it once when he was twelve and nearly took out a fence post. My grandfather was furious, but he calmed down after a slice of my grandmother's rhubarb pie. \n\nBut no, to answer your question... I haven't had any sudden spells of terror or anything physical like that. Just that constant, quiet worry I mentioned before.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tense in your muscles, like your body is stiff or tight, especially when you're not doing anything physically strenuous?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I do get terribly tight. By the time evening rolls around, my shoulders and my neck are just... they're so stiff. It's like I've been holding myself braced all day long without even realizing it. I'll be sitting there in my armchair—it's a lovely wingback, reupholstered just last year in this beautiful floral chintz. I found the fabric at that little shop downtown, the one next to the antique store. The owner, Margaret, has the most exquisite taste. She travels to England every spring to source her textiles. She brought back this gorgeous William Morris print once, but it was far too expensive for my budget. My daughter said I should have splurged, but you know how it is. You have to be sensible. \n\nAnyway, I'll be sitting in that chair, trying to read or, you know, plan out the activities for the garden center—we're doing a whole workshop on drought-resistant perennials next month, which I think is so important with the summers getting hotter—and I'll suddenly realize I'm clenching my jaw. Just biting down so hard. It gives me a bit of a headache sometimes, right at the temples. But yes, my muscles do get very tense, even when I'm just sitting quietly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I suppose it really is about a wide range of things. It's never just one thing, is it? My mind just keeps roaming all over the place. Like, I'll be thinking about the exhibition schedule for the local art society—I've been volunteering there on Tuesdays, though honestly, the lighting in that gallery is so poor, it gives me a headache if I stay too long. They really ought to upgrade to those new LED fixtures. My neighbor, Richard, put them in his kitchen and it's so bright you could perform surgery in there. He makes a wonderful sourdough bread, Richard does. He gave me a starter last winter, but I forgot to feed it and it just died right there in the jar. I felt so guilty. 

But anyway, my mind will just jump from the exhibition schedule to my daughter's travel plans—she's going to Italy in the fall, which sounds wonderful, but you hear such terrible things about pickpockets in Rome. I bought her one of those money belts, but she said it was too bulky. Kids never listen, do they? Even when they're forty. And then I'll start worrying about whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains start. We had a terrible leak three years ago that ruined the wallpaper in the guest room. It was a lovely damask pattern, very classic. 

So yes, it just goes from one thing to another, and these small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger. It's just exhausting, really.

It's never just one thing, you see. My mind just keeps roaming all over the place. Like yesterday, I started out fretting over the exhibition schedule at the historical society. And while I was thinking about that, suddenly I'm worrying about my daughter's travel plans. She's flying out next month, and you hear such dreadful things about lost luggage these days. My neighbor lost her suitcase in Rome and had to wear the same blouse for a week. A yellow blouse, too, which really washed her out, bless her heart. \n\nBut anyway, then I'm moving right along to thinking about whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains, because the oak tree in the front yard drops so many leaves. I don't even know why I get so worked up half the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, or trouble sleeping because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do... yes, the restlessness is quite noticeable. I'll be sitting right in my own living room, and I just feel so wound up. Like when I'm on the phone with my sister, Eleanor—I can't just sit still. I'm always tapping my fingers on the armrest, or I have to get up and straighten a picture frame or adjust the curtains. Sitting quietly just makes me feel terribly jumpy. \n\nEleanor is the same way, actually, though she’d never admit it. She’s always fussing over her hydrangeas. Oh, you should see her garden in the summer, it's magnificent. The blue ones, especially. She uses those coffee grounds to change the soil acidity, you know. I tried it once with my rhododendrons, but I don't think I drink enough coffee to make a difference. I mostly drink tea now. Earl Grey, usually. Though the last box I bought tasted a bit dusty. I wonder if they changed the bergamot supplier... but yes, what were we saying? Oh, the jumpiness. Yes, I just can't seem to settle down in my own house.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that cause you significant distress, or do you feel driven to perform certain behaviors or mental rituals to reduce the anxiety caused by these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I have... rituals, exactly. Or those intrusive thoughts you mentioned. It's really just the worrying, like I said. The fretting over things that might go wrong. I don't feel like I *have* to do things a certain way to stop the thoughts, like... like turning a doorknob three times or anything like that. \n\nMy cousin Arthur used to do things like that, you know. He had to check the stove was off exactly five times before he could leave the house. Every single time. It made him terribly late for my wedding. He missed the entire ceremony and only showed up for the reception, and even then, he was sweating profusely. Of course, it was July, and the air conditioning at the country club was on the fritz. They told us it would be fixed by the weekend, but you know how contractors are. Always promising the moon and delivering a puddle. We had to open all the windows, and then the mosquitoes got in. My poor mother had a bite right on her nose for all the photographs. She tried to cover it with powder, but it just made it look like a little pink mountain... but no, I don't have anything like Arthur had. Just the regular worrying, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying and feeling anxious happens more often than not, and has been going on like this for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, oh yes, definitely more often than not. I'd say it's been about seven months now. It all really started after my dear friend Margaret passed away. We were co-chairs of the historical society's annual gala, you see, and I just tried to keep all our little projects moving forward after she was gone. Margaret was always the organized one. She had this wonderful system of color-coded binders. Red for catering, blue for decorations, green for the silent auction... \n\nOh, the silent auction! Last year, someone donated a week at their timeshare in Boca Raton, and you wouldn't believe the bidding war between Mrs. Gable and the new dentist in town. Dr. Peterson, I think his name is. Very nice young man, though he did suggest I might need a night guard for my teeth. I told him I don't grind my teeth, but he said the enamel tells a different story. I suppose he'd know, being a dentist and all. He has such a lovely office, too—they play that very soothing classical music, cellos mostly. I've always loved the cello. My nephew tried to learn the cello in middle school, but the instrument was bigger than he was! He looked like a little turtle carrying its shell around. He gave it up for the clarinet eventually. Much easier to carry on the school bus. But yes, seven months of this constant worrying, ever since Margaret... I just haven't been able to turn my brain off since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety and worry are persistent and occur more days than not, even when there isn't an immediate reason for concern?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is like that. Even when things are perfectly fine, my mind just... keeps roaming. One minute I'm thinking about the historical society's exhibition schedule, and the next I'm fretting over my daughter's travel plans, or whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains. And it's like... these small things just start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger, you know? \n\nSpeaking of gutters, the man who came to clean them last year, Mr. Henderson—oh, he made such a mess of the flower beds right below the porch. Stepped right on my prize-winning begonias! I was so upset, I almost called his supervisor, but he was just a young boy, really. Looked like he couldn't have been more than nineteen. He told me he was saving up for community college. Wants to study marine biology, of all things. In Ohio! I asked him how he planned to study the ocean in the middle of the Midwest, and he said something about freshwater ecosystems. Which I suppose makes sense, with the lake right there. We used to go up to the lake every summer when the children were small. My husband would rent this little pontoon boat... oh, it was so peaceful. But now, yes, even when I'm just sitting quietly, the worry is just... always there, most days, even without a good reason.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worrying started to affect your daily life in significant ways, like making it hard to focus on tasks, causing problems at work or home, or affecting your relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't completely stopped me from doing things, but... it's definitely making things harder. I still keep up with my garden, of course, and the weekly book club. But I've noticed I'm just arriving less prepared. Last week we were reading that new historical fiction novel about the Tudor court, and I barely made it through the first three chapters. Usually, I have notes! \n\nAnd with the historical society... I've actually started turning down parts of the exhibition planning. It just feels so much harder to manage than it used to. I told them I couldn't handle the catering coordination this time around. Which is a shame, because I always use that lovely bakery on Elm Street. The one run by the two sisters? Oh, they make the most divine miniature fruit tarts. The pastry is so flaky, it just melts in your mouth. They use a special French butter, I believe. One of the sisters, Eleanor, went to culinary school in Paris. She met her husband there, a very handsome man who imports wine. They had a beautiful wedding in a chateau, or so I heard from Mrs. Gable. Mrs. Gable's daughter went to school with Eleanor, you see. Small world, isn't it? But yes, the planning just feels overwhelming now, so I've had to step back from some of my usual responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying tends to shift from one concern to another easily, even when those things aren't necessarily connected?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure what you mean by that. I mean, my worries are just... my worries, you know? They're all sort of jumbled up together in my head. I suppose they might seem disconnected to someone else, but to me, they're all just things that need my attention. \n\nLike yesterday, I was standing in line at the post office. I had to mail a package to my sister in Seattle—she's just had her kitchen remodeled, and I found the most darling set of vintage tea towels that match her new backsplash perfectly. Seafoam green, she went with. Very daring, I thought, but it looks lovely with the white cabinets. Anyway, I was standing there, and suddenly I started worrying about whether I'd left the iron on. I hadn't even been ironing! But then that made me think about my husband's dress shirts, and how the dry cleaner on Main Street always uses too much starch. The owner, Mr. Kim, is a sweetheart, though. He always gives my grandson a lollipop when we go in. Cherry, usually. Though last time it was grape, and little Tommy ended up with a purple tongue for hours! My daughter was not pleased, let me tell you. She's very strict about artificial dyes. Only natural fruit juices for her kids. Which I understand, I really do, but sometimes a little treat... \n\nOh, but you were asking about my worries shifting. I just... I don't know if they shift easily, or if they're just always there, all at once. I just haven't really noticed them jumping from one thing to another like that. It's more of a constant hum, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort that came on very quickly and included physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling short of breath?\n\nPatient: Sudden episodes? Like out of nowhere? No, I don't think so. I mean, my heart doesn't just start racing from fear or anything like that. The only time I really get sweaty or short of breath is if I'm out in the garden and it's terribly humid. \n\nYou know, like last July? Oh, it was just dreadful. We had that heatwave that lasted nearly three weeks. My poor hydrangeas were practically crisp by the end of it. I tried watering them twice a day, early morning and dusk, just like the Farmer's Almanac says to do. My grandfather swore by the Almanac. He used to farm corn down in southern Illinois. Hundreds of acres. He had this old green tractor that he'd let us ride on when we visited as children. It was so loud, it would make your teeth rattle, and the smell of the exhaust... well, it's funny the things you remember, isn't it? My brother actually tried to drive it once when he was twelve and nearly took out a fence post. My grandfather was furious, but he calmed down after a slice of my grandmother's rhubarb pie. \n\nBut no, to answer your question... I haven't had any sudden spells of terror or anything physical like that. Just that constant, quiet worry I mentioned before.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tense in your muscles, like your body is stiff or tight, especially when you're not doing anything physically strenuous?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I do get terribly tight. By the time evening rolls around, my shoulders and my neck are just... they're so stiff. It's like I've been holding myself braced all day long without even realizing it. I'll be sitting there in my armchair—it's a lovely wingback, reupholstered just last year in this beautiful floral chintz. I found the fabric at that little shop downtown, the one next to the antique store. The owner, Margaret, has the most exquisite taste. She travels to England every spring to source her textiles. She brought back this gorgeous William Morris print once, but it was far too expensive for my budget. My daughter said I should have splurged, but you know how it is. You have to be sensible. \n\nAnyway, I'll be sitting in that chair, trying to read or, you know, plan out the activities for the garden center—we're doing a whole workshop on drought-resistant perennials next month, which I think is so important with the summers getting hotter—and I'll suddenly realize I'm clenching my jaw. Just biting down so hard. It gives me a bit of a headache sometimes, right at the temples. But yes, my muscles do get very tense, even when I'm just sitting quietly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying is about a wide range of events or activities, rather than focusing on just one or two specific concerns?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it really is about a wide range of things. It's never just one thing, is it? My mind just keeps roaming all over the place. Like, I'll be thinking about the exhibition schedule for the local art society—I've been volunteering there on Tuesdays, though honestly, the lighting in that gallery is so poor, it gives me a headache if I stay too long. They really ought to upgrade to those new LED fixtures. My neighbor, Richard, put them in his kitchen and it's so bright you could perform surgery in there. He makes a wonderful sourdough bread, Richard does. He gave me a starter last winter, but I forgot to feed it and it just died right there in the jar. I felt so guilty. \n\nBut anyway, my mind will just jump from the exhibition schedule to my daughter's travel plans—she's going to Italy in the fall, which sounds wonderful, but you hear such terrible things about pickpockets in Rome. I bought her one of those money belts, but she said it was too bulky. Kids never listen, do they? Even when they're forty. And then I'll start worrying about whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains start. We had a terrible leak three years ago that ruined the wallpaper in the guest room. It was a lovely damask pattern, very classic. \n\nSo yes, it just goes from one thing to another, and these small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger. It's just exhausting, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "note": "",
  "is_final": false

you ever experienced sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort that came on very quickly and included physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling short of breath?\n\nPatient: Sudden episodes? We had that heatwave that lasted nearly three weeks. My poor hydrangeas were practically crisp by the end of it. I tried watering them twice a day, early morning and dusk, just like the Farmer's Almanac says to do. My grandfather swore by the Almanac. He used to farm corn down in southern Illinois. I'll be sitting there in my armchair—it's a lovely wingback, reupholstered just last year in this beautiful floral chintz. I found the fabric at that little shop downtown, the one next to the antique store. The owner, Margaret, has the most exquisite taste. She travels to England every spring to source her textiles. I felt so guilty. \n\nBut anyway, my mind will just jump from the exhibition schedule to my daughter's travel plans—she's going to Italy in the fall, which sounds wonderful, but you hear such terrible things about pickpockets in Rome. I bought her one of those money belts, but she said it was too bulky. Kids never listen, do they? Even when they're forty. And then I'll start worrying about whether the house gutters need fixing before the autumn rains start. We had a terrible leak three years ago that ruined the wallpaper in the guest room. It was a lovely damask pattern, very classic. \n\nSo yes, it just goes from one thing to another, and these small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger. It's just exhausting, really.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you can control your worrying, or does it often feel like your thoughts spiral beyond your control?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that's really the heart of the matter, isn't it? What brings me in is that I just can’t seem to turn off the worrying once it starts. It's like a faucet with a broken washer. You know, we had a plumber in last month for the kitchen sink—it was doing that drip, drip, drip thing that just drives you mad when you're trying to read in the living room. The plumber, a very nice young man named Tyler, said the pipes in this house are original to when it was built in the sixties. My husband and I bought it in 1984, right before the interest rates went completely crazy. We had to scrape together every penny for the down payment. My mother actually gave us a little loan, which was so generous of her. She was a very frugal woman, my mother. Grew up during the Depression, so she saved absolutely everything. Tin foil, rubber bands, those little plastic tabs that come on bread bags... 

But yes, the worrying. Even when I tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, and done all I reasonably can... it just keeps going. I can't control it at all. It just spirals.

Even when I tell myself, you know, I've made the list, I've checked all the dates, I've done everything I reasonably can. I've always been a bit of a planner, you see. Even back when I was working at the university—oh, the filing system I had there, it was a marvel. Everyone said so. My supervisor, Brenda, she used to say, \"Martha, if the building burns down, your desk will be the only thing organized enough to survive.\" She was a lovely woman, Brenda. Moved to Florida a few years ago. Or was it Arizona? Somewhere warm. I meant to send her a card for Christmas, but then the postage rates went up again, and I got so distracted trying to find my book of stamps... but yes, anyway, the worrying. It just... it doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's never just one thing, you see. My mind just keeps roaming all over the place. Like yesterday, I started out fretting over the exhibition schedule at the historical society. I volunteer there on Tuesdays, you know, and we're putting together a lovely display of vintage textiles. But the curator, David, he's terribly slow at finalizing the display cards. Yes, I just can't seem to settle down in my own house.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that cause you significant distress, or do you feel driven to perform certain behaviors or mental rituals to reduce the anxiety caused by these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I have... rituals, exactly. Or those intrusive thoughts you mentioned. It's really just the worrying, like I said. The fretting over things that might go wrong. I don't feel like I *have* to do things a certain way to stop the thoughts, like... like turning a doorknob three times or anything like that. \n\nMy cousin Arthur used to do things like that, you know. He had to check the stove was off exactly five times before he could leave the house. Every single time. It made him terribly late for my wedding. He missed the entire ceremony and only showed up for the reception, and even then, he was sweating profusely. Of course, it was July, and the air conditioning at the country club was on the fritz. They told us it would be fixed by the weekend, but you know how contractors are. Always promising the moon and delivering a puddle. We had to open all the windows, and then the mosquitoes got in. My poor mother had a bite right on her nose for all the photographs. She tried to cover it with powder, but it just made it look like a little pink mountain... but no, I don't have anything like Arthur had.
